Purpose of Filing 
10 
Q. 
What is the purpose of the Panel’s direct testimony in 
11 
this proceeding? 
12 
A. 
The purpose of our direct testimony is to provide a 
13 
brief overview of Orange and Rockland’s gas 
14 
distribution system. We will then discuss the future 
15 
of our system with an emphasis on the need to balance 
16 
progress toward reaching critical climate-related 
17 
policy goals with continuing to provide safe and 
18 
reliable service to customers at a reasonable price. 
19 
Next, we will describe the Company’s planned additions 
20 
to plant that are needed to continue to honor that 
21 
commitment. As part of the discussion on plant 
22

1 
replacement program and the progress made to date in 
2 
replacing leak prone pipe. Additionally, we will 
3 
discuss several technology investments that will 
4 
enhance the safety and reliability of our system. 
5 
Finally, we will discuss the Company’s planned 
6 
operation and maintenance (“O&M”) programs and 
7 
describe how these expenditures will increase safety, 
8 
improve the quality of the Company’s workforce, and 
9 
use technology to better track and protect the assets 
10

8 
Gas System Overview 
9 
Q.  Please provide a brief overview of the Company’s gas 
10 
business. 
11 
A. 
Based on data as of December 31, 2020, Orange and 
12 
Rockland serves approximately 139,563 natural gas 
13 
customers in a service area of 615 square miles, 
14 
encompassing 66 communities in Orange and Rockland 
15 
Counties. The area is served by 1,877 miles of gas 
16 
main ranging in size from 1¼-inch diameter to 20-inch 
17 
diameter, with 106,333 services to customer meters. Of 
18 
this total gas main, there is one mile of transmission 
19 
pipeline and 1,876 miles of distribution main. Orange 
20 
and Rockland’s distribution main is operated at 
21 
various pressures, including 79.0 miles operated at 
22 


--- Page 8 ---
 
ORANGE AND ROCKLAND UTILITIES, INC. 
DIRECT TESTIMONY OF 
GAS INFRASTRUCTURE AND OPERATIONS PANEL – GAS 
 
 
- 8 - 
extra high pressure (i.e., 125 pounds per square inch 
1 
gauge (“psig”) or more) and the remaining 1,797 miles 
2 
of main operated at high pressure. Gas Engineering and 
3 
Gas Operations headquarters are located at 390 West 
4 
Route 59 in Spring Valley, New York (“Spring Valley 
5 
Facility”). Because of the distances across the 
6 
Company’s service territory, O&R Gas Operations has 
7 
three field reporting locations to effectively manage 
8 
gas emergency response, maintenance and construction 
9 
in its Eastern and Northern Divisions. Crews for the 
10 
Eastern Division, which covers Rockland County, are 
11 
located at the Company’s Spring Valley Facility. Crews 
12 
for the Northern Division are located at the Company’s 
13 
Blooming Grove, New York, facility (“Blooming Grove 
14 
Facility”) and the Company’s Middletown, New York, 
15 
facility. Both the Spring Valley Facility and the 
16 
Blooming Grove Facility also have a customer service 
17 
center. In addition to the field reporting locations, 
18 
the Company has a training center located in Goshen, 
19 
New York (“Goshen Facility”) for operator 
20 
qualification of various gas field activities. The 
21 
Company has two gas control centers. The Gas Emergency 
22 


--- Page 9 ---
 
ORANGE AND ROCKLAND UTILITIES, INC. 
DIRECT TESTIMONY OF 
GAS INFRASTRUCTURE AND OPERATIONS PANEL – GAS 
 
 
- 9 - 
Response Center (“GERC”) is located in the Spring 
1 
Valley Operations Center. The GERC is the operating 
2 
authority for the gas distribution system where all 
3 
construction work is monitored on the system and leak 
4 
and emergency work is dispatched. The GERC is staffed 
5 
24 hours a day, seven days a week with O&R management 
6 
and union employees. The Company’s Gas Control Center 
7 
(”GCC”) is the operating authority for the O&R gas 
8 
system that operates at 125 psig or more. The GCC 
9 
monitors and controls system pressures and volumes 
10 
taken from gas suppliers. The GCC is co-located with 
11 
Con Edison’s GCC at Con Edison’s Van Nest facility, in 
12 
the Bronx, New York. Co-locating the O&R and Con 
13 
Edison GCCs allows the two companies to coordinate 
14 
purchases and transportation needs for gas deliveries 
15 
from joint portfolio purchases and to share 
16 
Supervisory Control and Data Acquisition (“SCADA”) 
17 
operating resources. The GCC is staffed 24 hours a 
18 
day, seven days a week with O&R management employees. 
19 
Q. 
Please describe the Company’s gas delivery system. 
20 
A.  With the exception of a few smaller areas, O&R 
21 
operates two separately integrated gas distribution 
22 


--- Page 10 ---
 
ORANGE AND ROCKLAND UTILITIES, INC. 
DIRECT TESTIMONY OF 
GAS INFRASTRUCTURE AND OPERATIONS PANEL – GAS 
 
 
- 10 - 
systems, one in Orange County and the other in 
1 
Rockland County. The interstate gas transportation 
2 
needs of O&R’s customers in Orange County are served 
3 
by Millennium Pipeline Company (“Millennium”) and 
4 
Columbia Gas Transmission through a series of 
5 
interconnection points off the Millennium transmission 
6 
pipeline that runs from west to east. Demand for gas 
7 
in Orange County, which is both rural and mountainous, 
8 
has largely followed the major highways that cross the 
9 
county with large and mostly undeveloped areas in 
10 
between. 
11 
In contrast, Rockland County is more densely developed 
12 
and interstate gas transportation needs for these 
13 
communities are provided by three different pipelines 
14 
(Millennium, Tennessee Gas Transmission Company 
15 
(“Tennessee”) and Algonquin Gas Transmission 
16 
(“Algonquin”)), to maximize capability and flexibility 
17 
to take and distribute gas supplies. Both Orange and 
18 
Rockland Counties have a networked high-pressure 
19 
distribution main system with redundancy provided by 
20 
the portfolio of 13 gate stations for flexible 
21 
operations in the event of an interruption at one of 
22 


--- Page 11 ---
 
ORANGE AND ROCKLAND UTILITIES, INC. 
DIRECT TESTIMONY OF 
GAS INFRASTRUCTURE AND OPERATIONS PANEL – GAS 
 
 
- 11 - 
the gate stations or in a feeder line. In addition, 
1 
our systems have several larger district regulating 
2 
stations, and 17 remote operated valves (“ROVs”) on 
3 
the Company’s feeder lines, which are centrally 
4 
monitored from the O&R GCC.

Commitment to State Policy Goals 
7 
Q. 
Is the Company committed to supporting state policy 
8 
goals, including any requirements that the State 
9 
adopts pursuant to the Climate Leadership and 
10 
Community Protection Act (“CLCPA”)? 
11 
A. 
Yes, the Company has adopted its own Clean Energy 
12 
Commitment and supports state climate change and clean 
13 
energy policy goals, including carbon emissions 
14 
reductions.  Additional information on the Company’s 
15 
Clean Energy Commitment is available at:  
16 
https://www.oru.com/en/our-energy-future/our-energy-
17 
vision/our-energy-future-commitment 
18 
Q. 
Please describe some of the steps the Company has 
19 
taken or plans to take that demonstrate its 
20 
commitment. 
21 


--- Page 12 ---
 
ORANGE AND ROCKLAND UTILITIES, INC. 
DIRECT TESTIMONY OF 
GAS INFRASTRUCTURE AND OPERATIONS PANEL – GAS 
 
 
- 12 - 
A. 
The following are examples of actions the Company has 
1 
taken or plans to take to achieve New York’s clean 
2 
energy policy goals: 
3 
 
Reduce the carbon intensity of gas transmission and 
4 
distribution by lowering emissions from operations 
5 
(e.g., main replacement program); 
6 
 
Enhance awareness and education of low carbon 
7 
heating alternatives and provide incentives to 
8 
reduce the upfront costs of installing these 
9 
technologies (e.g., cold climate air-source heat 
10 
pumps and ground-source heat pumps); 
11 
 
Help customers optimize their energy use (and 
12 
thereby reduce their carbon footprint) through 
13 
energy efficiency and demand response; 
14 
 
Promote Non-Pipeline Alternatives (“NPAs”) to allow 
15 
for flexibility in how we plan our future;  
16 
 
Support efforts by developers in our service 
17 
territory to pursue renewable natural gas (“RNG”) 
18 
as a complement to energy efficiency 
19 
/electrification and potential for long-term 
20 
targeted uses (e.g., transport, industrial, 
21 


--- Page 13 ---
 
ORANGE AND ROCKLAND UTILITIES, INC. 
DIRECT TESTIMONY OF 
GAS INFRASTRUCTURE AND OPERATIONS PANEL – GAS 
 
 
- 13 - 
electric generation) as a means to reduce the net 
1 
carbon impact of gas supply by establishing a 
2 
standard gas system interconnection process for RNG 
3 
facilities; and 
4 
 
Continue active participation with the joint 
5 
utilities group in the Commission’s state-wide Gas 
6 
Planning Proceeding (Case 20-G-0131) to examine 
7 
utility planning and operational practices. 
8

 
Asset management; 
18 
 
Emergency response activations; 
19 
 
Examination of worker safety protocols; and 
20 
 
Climate risk governance.

Q. 
What is the Company’s role in relation to the CCIP? 
1 
A.   O&R has representation on the Climate Risk and 
2

4

6 
Q. 
What are the general challenges the Company 
7 
anticipates with respect to convincing customers to 
8 
consider and adopt natural gas alternatives to meet 
9 
their energy needs? 
10 
A. 
Natural gas remains a reliable, abundant, low-cost 
11 
fuel that residential customers rely upon for their 
12 
space heating, water heating and cooking needs.  
13 
Commercial and industrial customers also use natural 
14 
gas for the purposes described above, as well as for 
15 
industrial processes.  These businesses, and the jobs 
16 
they produce, sustain and contribute to further 
17 
economic development in the communities we serve.  
18 
Q. 
Among these general challenges, is there a particular 
19 
obstacle to greater customer adoption of natural gas 
20 
alternatives? 
21 


--- Page 15 ---
 
ORANGE AND ROCKLAND UTILITIES, INC. 
DIRECT TESTIMONY OF 
GAS INFRASTRUCTURE AND OPERATIONS PANEL – GAS 
 
 
- 15 - 
A. 
Yes, the upfront price of alternatives relative to 
1 
natural gas remains the main obstacle to greater 
2 
customer adoption. Although improved customer 
3 
education regarding total cost of ownership of natural 
4 
gas alternatives is important and necessary to raise 
5 
customer awareness, it has been the Company’s 
6 
experience that the fundamental economic benefits of 
7 
gas compared to other alternatives still present a 
8 
significant hurdle. For example, even with rebates it 
9 
can take up to 5 years for a cold-climate air-sourced 
10 
heat pump to be on par with the total cost of 
11 
ownership of natural gas.1  Heat pumps are, however, 
12 
economically competitive much sooner with fuel oil 
13 
when natural gas service is not available. 
14 
Q. 
Can the Company force gas customers to convert from 
15 
natural gas service to alternative lower-carbon energy 
16 
sources? 
17 
A. 
No. It is our understanding that the Company has a 
18 
statutory obligation under the New York Public Service 
19 
 
1 See https://www.nyserda.ny.gov/-
/media/Files/Publications/PPSER/NYSERDA/18-44-HeatPump.pdf at pages 55-
56.

GAS INFRASTRUCTURE AND OPERATIONS PANEL – GAS 
 
 
- 16 - 
Law and Transportation Corporation Law to provide 
1 
natural gas service in our service territory, subject 
2 
to the conditions in the Company’s gas tariff. In 
3 
addition, and related to our obligation to serve, the 
4 
Company is required to distribute natural gas in a 
5 
safe and reliable manner. 
6 
Q. 
What are the Company’s expectations regarding the 
7 
demand for natural gas in the near to medium term? 
8 
A. 
Given customers’ demonstrated preference for natural 
9 
gas service and the existing statutory obligation to 
10 
serve, the Company expects that natural gas will 
11 
continue to serve many of the energy needs of our 
12 
customers in the near- to medium-term. Please see the 
13 
direct testimony of the Company’s Gas Forecasting 
14 
Panel for a more detailed discussion of the Company’s 
15 
forecast for future gas demand.  
16 
Q. 
What is the basis for the level of capital investment 
17 
and incremental O&M costs you have proposed in this 
18 
case? 
19 
A. 
Because it is expected that the Company will continue 
20 
to provide natural gas service to meet customers’ 
21 
energy needs, continued investment is needed to do so 
22 


--- Page 17 ---
 
ORANGE AND ROCKLAND UTILITIES, INC. 
DIRECT TESTIMONY OF 
GAS INFRASTRUCTURE AND OPERATIONS PANEL – GAS 
 
 
- 17 - 
in a safe, reliable and environmentally considerate 
1 
manner.  Targeted prudent investment in a safe and 
2 
reliable gas distribution system, as well as cost-
3 
effective operation and maintenance of the gas 
4 
distribution system is still required. These 
5 
investments can also result in lowering emissions of 
6 
methane through replacement of leak prone pipe.  While 
7 
the Company expects to be a provider of safe and 
8 
reliable natural gas service well beyond the term of 
9 
this one-year rate filing and any multi-year rate 
10 
plan, the Company also recognizes the need to 
11 
implement its strategy to meet its own Clean Energy 
12

13 
goals.  To that end, the Company will, among other 
14 
measures, explore investments in shorter-term assets 
15 
such as NPAs to diversify its portfolio and mitigate 
16 
risks while maintaining reliability. As discussed by 
17 
the Company’s Depreciation Panel, the Company is also 
18 
proposing to shorten depreciation lives of longer-
19 
lived gas assets to take initial steps towards 
20 
reflecting the State’s decarbonization goal. 
21

Q. 
Going forward, what are the guiding principles that 
1 
will shape the Company’s plan for its future gas 
2 
business? 
3 
A. 
The Company will apply the following guiding 
4 
principles to its future gas business that will 
5 
maintain the integrity and reliability of the gas 
6 
system while supporting clean energy policy goals: 
7 
 
Deliver safe and reliable service to customers at a 
8 
reasonable cost, as part of our statutory 
9 
obligation to serve; 
10 
 
Decarbonize by reducing the current level of 
11 
emission intensity of operations and exploring new 
12 
cleaner technologies; 
13 
 
Increase customer awareness of and education on 
14 
lower carbon heating alternatives and aggressively 
15 
promote the Company’s heat pump program; and 
16 
 
Advocate for rate reform and legislative changes 
17 
that enable utility support of clean energy targets 
18 
and help customers transition to a clean energy 
19 
future. 
20 
 
21

Q. 
Can you please elaborate further on supply side 
3 
initiatives? 
4 
A. 
Yes. The Company is committed to monitoring the 
5 
viability and supporting the adoption of alternatives 
6 
to traditional methane such as RNG. By prudently 
7 
investing at the levels proposed in this case, the 
8 
Company’s distribution system will be prepared to 
9 
deliver RNG to customers should RNG projects 
10 
interconnect to the Company’s gas distribution system.  
11 
In the longer term, ‘green’ hydrogen (hydrogen 
12 
produced through the electrolysis of water using 
13 
renewable electricity to power the process) is being 
14 
explored as a potential future source of energy in 
15 
gaseous form, and could be a cost-effective way, along 
16 
with renewable electricity, to meet winter heating 
17 
peaks in the future.  Failure to invest in and 
18 
maintain the gas distribution system may preclude the 
19 
use of these future alternatives. 
20

7

8

Company’s regulatory and financial constraints.

proposed for purchase, construction, and retirement of

capital assets in the upcoming budget year. The 
14

15 
approval in the first half of each calendar year. Any 
16 
capital project for which funds are sought for the 
17 
upcoming budget year is required to be evaluated 
18 
during the Capital Optimization Process. The process 
19

approval by the O&R Capital Governance Committee (“O&R

1

3

4

5 
approximately three miles per year to 18 miles per 
6 
year, targeting two-inch bare steel main. At the same 
7 
time, it also expanded its replacement efforts to 
8 
include larger diameter bare steel, Aldyl plastic and 
9 
cast iron pipe. For RY1, RY2 and RY3, almost 100% of 
10 
pipe replacement is conducted within blanket projects.

18

prone pipe and prioritized for replacement because the

Q.

10

11 
A.

12 
Protection (“CP”) Department identified that have 
13 
issues with exposed steel gas mains on bridges, 
14 
including surface rust and corrosion, coating failure 
15 
and disbondment, pitting and flaking rust at the 
16 
interface between the pipe and some hanger supports 
17 
and severe corrosion of the steel mains where they 
18 
penetrate the concrete abutment walls on both ends of 
19 
the bridge. CP also found the following issues with 
20 
the hangers supporting the steel gas main pipe to the 
21 
bridge: missing or broken supports and heavy surface 
22 


--- Page 36 ---
 
ORANGE AND ROCKLAND UTILITIES, INC. 
DIRECT TESTIMONY OF 
GAS INFRASTRUCTURE AND OPERATIONS PANEL – GAS 
 
 
- 36 - 
corrosion of the supports. In all three locations, 
1 
existing exposed steel gas mains should be replaced 
2 
with a single new 8” plastic main that will be 
3 
horizontally directionally drilled under the New York 
4 
State Thruway. Installing the main underground allows 
5 
the Company to use high density polyethylene plastic 
6 
main, instead of the steel main required for a main 
7 
installed on a bridge, thereby preventing future 
8 
corrosion issues. It will increase the safety and 
9 
reliability of the system by replacing outdated 
10 
infrastructure while eliminating corrosion issues and 
11 
reducing the likelihood of third-party damage. For 
12 
additional information please see the white paper 
13 
included in Exhibit GIOP–1.

AMI Natural Gas Detector Program  
1 
Q. 
Please describe the Company’s progress with advanced 
2 
metering infrastructure (“AMI”) natural gas detectors 
3 
(“NGDs”). 
4 
A. 
NGDs are safety devices typically installed in 
5 
customers’ indoor gas meter rooms near the head of 
6 
service. The NGDs are designed to provide continuous 
7 
monitoring of methane. When an AMI-enabled NGD alarms 
8 
(10% of the Lower Explosive Limit (“LEL”)), the signal 
9 
is transmitted through the AMI network to the GERC. 
10 
The GERC will then dispatch a Gas Service 
11 
Troubleshooter to respond to the potential gas leak 
12 
using normal leak response protocols. The GERC will 
13 
also notify the applicable Fire Department. These 
14 
detectors will help reduce risk and prevent natural 
15 
gas incidents. In addition to enhancing safety, these 
16 
devices will also help to reduce fugitive emissions of 
17 
methane.  
18 
Q. 
What is Orange and Rocklands plan for installing AMI 
19 
NGDs? 
20 
A. 
The Company proposes to start a multi-year program to 
21 
target locations within business districts to install 
22 


--- Page 42 ---
 
ORANGE AND ROCKLAND UTILITIES, INC. 
DIRECT TESTIMONY OF 
GAS INFRASTRUCTURE AND OPERATIONS PANEL – GAS 
 
 
- 42 - 
these devices. The Company estimates installing these 
1 
devices in approximately 460 locations per year, for a 
2 
total of 1,400 units installed by the end of 2024 
3 
(RY3). The Orange and Rockland and Con Edison pilot 
4 
programs have demonstrated that the AMI system is 
5 
capable of generating alarms into emergency centers, 
6 
allowing the Company to use this information 
7 
successfully to respond to a customer location. For 
8 
additional information please see the white paper 
9 
included in Exhibit GIOP–1. 
10

15

16

17 
will not only reduce system risk, but also avoid the 
18 
need to confirm the non-High Consequence Area (“HCA”) 
19 
status of each area.  Such replacement also avoids 
20 
integrity assessments if an HCA were to develop, as 
21 
the replaced pipe in the stations would no longer fall 
22

5

6 
A.

7

8

9 
wrapped steel pipe, related fittings, and 12-inch 
10

11

12 
the Gas Control Center, giving the Company the ability 
13 
to remotely isolate a section of the extra high 
14 
pressure system in the event of a major pipeline 
15 
incident.  By having this ability, the Company is able 
16 
to minimize the hazard of blowing gas and its 
17 
consequences (both safety and environmental). For 
18 
additional information please see the white paper

included in Exhibit GIOP–1.

Millennium Pipeline Backfeed 
14 
Q.  Are there any other system reliability projects you 
15 
would like to discuss? 
16 
A.  Yes, O&R's Orange County customers are directly fed 
17 
from gate stations connected to the Millennium 
18 
transmission pipeline. Should there be an interruption 
19 
of service (e.g., damage, equipment failure, supply 
20 
constraint) upstream on the Millennium’s pipeline it 
21 
could impact all or most of the approximately 45,000 
22 


--- Page 52 ---
 
ORANGE AND ROCKLAND UTILITIES, INC. 
DIRECT TESTIMONY OF 
GAS INFRASTRUCTURE AND OPERATIONS PANEL – GAS 
 
 
- 52 - 
Orange County customers. This places customers at risk 
1 
for losing service, where restoration potentially 
2 
could take multiple days or weeks, depending on the 
3 
event. In particular, an interruption of service in 
4 
cold weather (below freezing) could result in health 
5 
and safety emergencies, causing customers to relocate 
6 
from their homes or business, and risking damage to 
7 
premises if internal water pipes freeze. 
8 
Q.  What does O&R recommend to mitigate this type of 
9 
potential large-scale gas outage? 
10 
A.  The Company has been exploring establishing a backfeed 
11 
into the Millennium pipeline. Currently, the 
12 
Millennium pipeline has a physical interconnection 
13 
point in Ramapo, New York with the Algonquin pipeline. 
14 
During normal operation, Millennium supplies gas to 
15 
Algonquin at this interconnection and, to the 
16 
Company's knowledge, Algonquin currently cannot supply 
17 
gas to Millennium in the event there was an 
18 
interruption of service on Millennium. O&R has held 
19 
preliminary discussion with Millennium, which has 
20 
expressed a willingness to review this further. 
21 
Assuming a tentative agreement can be reached with 
22 


--- Page 53 ---
 
ORANGE AND ROCKLAND UTILITIES, INC. 
DIRECT TESTIMONY OF 
GAS INFRASTRUCTURE AND OPERATIONS PANEL – GAS 
 
 
- 53 - 
Millennium, the Company would then reach out to seek 
1 
Algonquin’s agreement. If an agreement can be reached 
2 
with both pipelines O&R will advise Staff and discuss 
3 
next steps. 
4 
Q.  What are the estimated costs of this project?  
5 
A.  The preliminary cost estimate is up to $5 million 
6 
dollars to build a physical back-feed.  
7 
Q. 
How does the Company propose to recover the costs 
8 
associated with this project? 
9 
A. 
Because the equipment needed for this project would 
10 
not be owned by the Company, but instead would be 
11 
owned and operated by Millennium and/or Algonquin, the 
12 
Company would incur the cost as a surcharge to its 
13 
existing pipeline contracts. Cost recovery from 
14 
customer would therefore occur through the cost of 
15 
gas.

Q. 
Please explain the change in the definition of 
15 
“Service Line” adopted by the Commission. 
16 
A. 
On April 2, 2015, in Case No. 14-G-0357, the 
17 
Commission adopted an amendment to its Gas Safety 
18 
Regulations (16 NYCRR 255.3(a)(29)), which revised the 
19 
Commission’s service line definition to align with the 
20 
federal definition. This change alters long-standing 
21 
practice in New York State by extending the 
22 


--- Page 57 ---
 
ORANGE AND ROCKLAND UTILITIES, INC. 
DIRECT TESTIMONY OF 
GAS INFRASTRUCTURE AND OPERATIONS PANEL – GAS 
 
 
- 57 - 
Commission’s jurisdiction over gas piping inside 
1 
buildings up to the customer meter (or to the customer 
2 
piping if there is no meter). The primary practical 
3 
effect of this change is to extend the utility’s gas 
4 
leak survey and corrosion inspection obligations to 
5 
cover the additional piping now included within the 
6 
revised definition of “service line.” In addition, the 
7 
Commission initiated a separate proceeding (Case No. 
8 
15-G-0244) in order to develop a State implementation 
9 
framework that considers the practical application of, 
10 
primarily, leakage survey and corrosion inspection 
11 
requirements. On April 20, 2017, the Commission issued 
12 
an Order in Case 15-G-0244 that immediately 
13 
implemented the expanded leak survey and corrosion 
14 
inspection requirements.  
15 
Q. 
What changes has the Company made in response to the 
16 
new service line definition? 
17 
A. 
In response, the Company updated its Work Procedure 
18 
4063 and expanded its gas service line surveys and

12basis point incentive change. 
2 
Q. 
What is the basis for this modification? 
3 
A.

4

5 
backlog over the last several years.  By shifting the 
6 
incentive from total leaks to repairable leaks, the 
7

9

10 
total leak backlog. In addition, the Company schedules 
11 
the majority of all initial leak repairs within 2-3 
12 
weeks of discovery.

 1. Project / Program Summary

Project/Program Title: Main Replacement Program: Increase to Bare Steel Main Replacement

Work Description: 
The Company’s main replacement program has two blankets (i.e., Bare steel and Aldyl) that cover the 
costs relating to gas main replacement pursuant to the terms of the current gas rate plan. The objective 
of this gas main replacement program is to eliminate the riskiest gas main in the gas system. The 
removal of this gas main will improve pipeline integrity, safety, and reliability. The replacement of 
bare steel mains also contributes towards a reduction in methane emissions.  
 
The Bare Steel Main Replacement blanket is used for the replacement of leak prone bare steel gas main. 
Bare steel mains were installed in Orange and Rockland’s gas distribution system prior to 1962, range 
in sizes from 2” to 12” in diameter and are not protected with any type of cathodic protection or 
coating material. Orange and Rockland has found bare steel pipe is susceptible to corrosion and has 
identified it as leak prone pipe. The Company evaluates all Leak Prone Pipe on an on-going basis for 
replacement prioritization. Gas Engineering reviews all in-coming leaks and other factors to determine 
how the pipe should be prioritized for replacement. More specifically, these other factors include the 
DIMP risk model and subject matter expert input based on field experiences. Orange and Rockland’s 
Replacement of  bare steel gas mains occurs on both a planned and emergent basis.  Since Orange and 
Rockland’s last gas rate filing (Case 18-G-0068), the Company has experienced an increase in projects 
on state and county roads, as well as an increase in the installation of larger size gas mains, which has 
increased estimates for Bare Steel Main Replacement projects.  
 
 
Justification Summary: 
An increase in funding for the Bare Steel Main Replacement blanket is required in order to replace 
more costly leak prone bare steel pipe in the portfolio. These projects include main replacement 
projects on state and county roads, projects that require the installation of large diameter main 
installations, as well as upgrading system pressures during main installations  


--- Page 71 ---
 
 
EXHIBIT____ (GIOP-1) 
 
 
PAGE 4 OF 141 
 
 
 
4 
 
Large diameter main installations (e.g., 12”, 8” & 6” high density plastic pipe) are more expensive per 
linear foot than the more common 2” PLX installations, and account for approximately 41 miles of the 
total Bare Steel Main Replacement still required to be completed for the program. That equates to 
approximately 5 miles per year out of the total 20 miles per year (approximately 25%) of main 
replacement required annually through the end of the program. Main replacement projects on state 
and county roadways have much higher costs per foot, typically, these types of projects cost 
approximately 55% more than similar type projects on town roadways. Factors contributing to 
increased costs include: 1) Pipe depth required is 5ft ,where code requires 3ft, 2)  12" deep concrete 
road bed that must be excavated through the asphalt, require excavations be made to the nearest 
concrete road bed joint, which can increase the width of the excavation by 2' - 3' (State wants to 
maintain the integrity and structural strength of the concrete road bed), 3) Backfill/restoration material 
required is more expensive material (Super Pave - stronger asphalt), and 4) Consultant is required in 
order to develop Surveys & Scaled Engineering Drawings. 
 
The table below lists identified projects that fall within this category. The additional requirements puts 
a strain on scheduling and available funding within the blanket. The additional funding needed for the 
Bare Steel Main Replacement blanket will allow for the scheduling and completion of these projects by 
the Company.

The objective of this gas main replacement program is to eliminate the riskiest gas main in the 
Company’s system. The removal of this gas main will improve pipeline integrity, safety, and 
reliability.  
 
 
Relationship to 5-Year and Long-Range Plans and Enterprise Risk Management Strategy 
This program mitigates the Enterprise Risk of a Gas Distribution System Event by replacing bare steel 
gas mains with plastic and/or protected steel. They are proven to be more resilient and will provide 
many years of additional service. Planned main replacement is performed for multiple reasons such as 
a work coordination opportunity or being highly ranked in our risk model. Additionally, ever 
changing municipal paving schedules may require the Company to re-prioritize work plans to adapt to 
emerging conditions. Other emergent conditions such as irreparable leaks can lead to expedited 
replacement.  
 
 
 
2. Supplemental Information 
 
Alternatives 
 
Alternative 1 description and reason for rejection 
The Company could focus on less expensive projects first. However, performing less expensive main 
replacement projects to meet budget constraints would move the more expensive projects to the end of 
the program creating future budget constraints. In addition, this would reduce flexibility in 
scheduling, where timely coordination with state and county officials which help to keep costs down.  
Eventually, to complete all bare steel main replacement within the gas system, the 41 miles of large 
diameter leak prone pipe along with the projects located on state and county roadway will need to be 
replaced in order to complete the elimination of all bare steel main pipe within the system.   
 
 
Alternative 2 description and reason for rejection 
 
 
 
Alternative 3 description and reason for rejection 
 
 
 
Risk of No Action 
 
Risk 1 
This is a rate case performance indicator and therefore a penalty will be assessed for failing to meet the 
target.

Risk 2 
The 41 miles of large diameter leak prone pipe along with the projects located on state and county 
roadway keep being pushed off due to funding and it does not get properly replaced in time.  
 
 
Risk 3 
 
Non-Financial Benefits 
 
• 
Increased safety and reliability.  Specifically, mitigation of Company Enterprise Risks such as 
Gas Distribution Event, and Large Scale Gas Customer Outage, as well as Orange and 
Rockland’s Enterprise Risk of Gas System Fire Explosion.  
• 
Increased customer satisfaction resulting from fewer required leak repairs

4. Basis for estimate 
This estimate was based on similar projects completed in the past. 
It is estimated that it will cost the Company an additional $7,680,000 to complete the eleven projects 
identified on state and county roadways compared to doing the same projects on a town or local 
roadway. The Company plans to spend an additional $1,920,000 per year, for the next four years to 
complete this list of projects.    
 
 
5. Conclusion 
The objective of this gas main replacement program is to eliminate the riskiest gas main in the 
Company’s system. The removal of this gas main will improve pipeline integrity, safety, and 
reliability. This program mitigates the Enterprise Risk of a Gas Distribution System Event.  
 
Eventually, the 41 miles of large diameter leak prone pipe along with the projects located on state and 
county roadway will need to be replaced in order to eliminate this leak prone pipe.

Gas Operations and Engineering 
 2022-2024 
 1. Project / Program Summary 
 
Type:  ☐ Project  ☒ Program  
Category:  ☒ Capital  ☐ O&M 
Work Plan Category:  ☒ Regulatory Mandated  ☐ Operationally Required  ☐ Strategic 
Project/Program Title: Main Replacement Program: Supplemental Increase due to Restoration Costs 
& Contractor Rates 
Project/Program Manager: Victoria Sorvillo 
Project/Program Number (Level 1): 
Status:  ☐ Initiation  ☐ Planning  ☐ Execution  ☒ On-going  ☐  ☐ Other: ___________ 
Estimated Start Date: 1/1/2022 
Estimated Date In Service: 12/31/2024 
C. Total Funding Request ($000)  
Capital:  $4,200 
O&M:  
 
D.  
☐ 5-Year Gross Cost Savings ($000) 
☐ 5-Year Gross Cost Avoidance ($000) 
O&M: 
Capital: 
E. 5-Year Ongoing Maintenance Expense ($000) 
O&M: 
Capital: 
F. Investment Payback Period:   
(Years/months) (If applicable) 
Work Description: 
 
The Company’s Main Replacement Program includes two blankets (i.e., Bare steel and Aldyl) that 
cover the costs relating to eliminating leak prone pipe pursuant to the terms of the current gas rate 
plan. The objective of this gas main replacement program is to eliminate the riskiest gas main in the 
system, which is referred to as leak prone pipe. All leak prone pipe is evaluated on an on-going basis 
for replacement prioritization. Gas Engineering reviews all in-coming leaks to determine how the pipe 
should be prioritized for replacement and uses other tools to provide insight into the prioritization of 
replacement, such as the Distribution Integrity Management Plan (“DIMP”) Risk Model, and Subject 
Matter Expert input based on field experiences. Replacement of these gas mains can occur on both a 
planned and emergent basis.  
 
The removal of this leak prone pipe will improve pipeline integrity, safety, and reliability within the 
gas distribution system for the Company.  
 
Since the most recent rate case, the Company has experienced an increase in 
contractor costs for gas main installations and an increase in requirements for 
paving/restoration. (e.g., 6 feet wide lane increased to 12 feet lane to pave over 
trench). Additional funding is being requested to increase the Main Replacement 
Blanket in order to cover these increased costs.  
 
 
Justification Summary: 
 


--- Page 77 ---
 
 
EXHIBIT____ (GIOP-1) 
 
 
PAGE 10 OF 141 
 
 
 
10 
Contractor costs for gas main replacements have increased since our last rate case filing.  The increased 
costs can be attributed to Operator Qualification compliance, increased bond and liability insurance 
requirements.  These increases have been partially offset by recent contracts, which have been bid for 
five-year terms resulting in more favorable pricing compared to shorter duration projects. 
 
The current contracts for all existing gas contractors expire in 2022. The Company will seek 
competitive bids to attain new gas contracts. It is expected that the new contracts will be at least 3% 
higher than current rates based on similar bids performed in the past along with inflation. This will 
add at least $700,000 annually toward the Company’s blankets.   
 
Historically, the requirement for trench restoration and paving were to just mill and pave a gas trench 
anywhere from 4’-6’ wide from the local Municipalities.  Since the most recent rate case filing, many 
local Municipalities have increased the width for paving and restoration to one lane (12’ wide). Based 
on the Company’s yearly Main Replacement goal of completing at least 22 miles (116,000 linear feet), 
this translates into an additional $1.7M annually towards the Company’s blankets. Potentially more if 
the list of Municipalities adopting these requirements grows.  
 
 
Relationship to 5-Year and Long-Range Plans and Enterprise Risk Management Strategy 
 
The gas main replacement program is predominantly completed utilizing gas contractors and paving 
restoration is required in all municipalities. The gas main replacement program cannot be completed 
without contractors or restoration.

trench restoration and paving.

Work Description: 
 
Upon notice of public improvement, city, and municipal construction activities, public utilities must 
relocate or protect any interfering utilities. Whenever possible, O&R Gas Engineering strategically 
performs advance relocation of utilities to avoid future interference with municipal construction 
projects.   
 
The Company’s Municipal blanket covers the costs of activities associated with these types of projects 
where the Company must relocate or modify existing gas main in order to accommodate work or 
structures being installed  by various municipalities such as: New York State Department of 
Transportation, New York State Thruway Authority, Rockland County Department of Public Works, 
Orange County Department of Public Works, along with various Cities, Towns, and Villages through 
the Rockland and Orange County territories.   
 
There has been an increase in required municipal project spending in 2020, and it is being forecasted to 
continue for the next several years. For 2020, the Municipal spend is expected to be approximately 
$6.7M, and 2021 is currently projected to be approximately $7.5M. This increase is due to the rise in the 
amount of work being conducted by the municipalities. The three-year average for 2019 to 2021 is 
expected to be approximately $5.4M per year. Based on this trend and the current list of municipal 
projects already planned, additional funding will be needed. for 2022 – 2024.  This additional funding 
to the Municipal Blanket should provide adequate funding to complete mandatory Municipal projects.

Justification Summary: 
 
The Company has seen a significant increase in utility interference with public improvement and 
municipal construction activities this past year, and it is being forecasted to continue for the next 
several years. For 2020, the Municipal spend is expected to be approximately $6.7M, and 2021 is 
currently projected to be $7.5M.  
 
This is based on both known municipal projects and historical trends of interference projects that may 
arise due to unforeseeable municipal construction activity (e.g., water main break, structural damage, 
etc.). The Company has been made aware of the municipal projects listed below, and as the norm, 
expects more projects to be submitted throughout the year with shorter timeframes for completion.

Gas Operations and Engineering 
 2022-2024 
 1. Project / Program Summary 
 
Type:  ☒ Project  ☐ Program  
Category:  ☒ Capital  ☐ O&M 
Work Plan Category:  ☐ Regulatory Mandated  ☒ Operationally Required  ☐ Strategic 
Project/Program Title: Cathodic Protection Projects for Exposed Main on Bridges 
Project/Program Manager: Giovanni Coppa 
Project/Program Number (Level 1): 
Status:  ☒ Initiation  ☐ Planning  ☐ Execution  ☐ On-going  ☐  ☐ Other: ___________ 
Estimated Start Date: 6/15/2022 
Estimated Date In Service: 12/31/2024 
G. Total Funding Request ($000)  
Capital: $2,230.0 
O&M:  
 
H.  
☐ 5-Year Gross Cost Savings ($000) 
☒ 5-Year Gross Cost Avoidance ($000) 
O&M: $457.5 
Capital: 
I. 
5-Year Ongoing Maintenance Expense ($000) 
O&M: 
Capital: 
J. 
Investment Payback Period:   
(Years/months) (If applicable) 
Work Description: 
The Company’s Cathodic Protection (“CP”) Department identified three locations with issues on 
exposed steel gas mains on bridges.  
 
CP conducted a survey of all three locations and found the following issues on the steel main: Surface 
rust & corrosion, coating failure and disbondment, pitting and flaking rust at the interface between the 
pipe and some hanger supports. CP also found the following issues with the hangers supporting the 
steel gas main pipe to the bridge: missing or broken supports and heavy surface corrosion of the 
supports. During the inspection, CP also found severe corrosion of the steel mains where they 
penetrate the concrete abutment walls on both ends of the bridge.  
 
At all three locations the existing exposed steel gas mains should be replaced with a single new 8” high 
density polyethylene plastic main that will be Horizontally Directionally Drilled under the New York 
State Thruway. Installing the main underground allows the Company to use high density polyethylene 
plastic main, instead of the steel main required for a main installed on a bridge, preventing future 
corrosion issues. This is a long-term solution that will rectify the corrosion issue with the steel main 
and hanger supports on the bridge. It will also save maintenance costs because the Company would no 
longer be required to inspect/survey the main as part of the Exposed Main Inspection program. It will 
increase the Safety and Reliability of the system by replacing outdated infrastructure while eliminating 
the corrosion issues and also reducing the likelihood of third-party damage.   
 
 
First Location – Route 59 in Monsey, NY:    
There are two existing exposed gas mains under the bridge located on Route 59 in Monsey, NY that 
spans over the New York State Thruway. The first exposed gas main is 8” Wrapped Steel that was 
installed in 1974. The second exposed gas main is 6” Wrapped Steel, that was installed in 1973.  


--- Page 87 ---
 
 
EXHIBIT____ (GIOP-1) 
 
 
PAGE 20 OF 141 
 
 
 
20 
 
 
Second Location – South Franklin St (Willow St) in South Nyack, NY:    
There is an existing exposed gas main under the bridge located on South Franklin St in South Nyack, 
NY that spans over the New York State Thruway. The exposed gas main is 6” Bare Steel that was 
installed in 1954.  
 
 
Third Location – Mountainview Ave in Central Nyack, NY:    
There is an existing exposed gas main under the bridge located on Mountainview Ave in Central 
Nyack, NY that spans over the New York State Thruway. The exposed gas main is 4” Wrapped Steel 
and lies within a 6” steel casing. Both were installed in 1997.  
 
 
 
 
 
Justification Summary: 
Existing exposed gas mains in Orange and Rockland’s gas distribution system are inspected by the 
Cathodic Protection Department for corrosion issues, broken or corroded supports, leaks, and various 
other issues. By replacing the existing exposed gas mains at these three locations, the Company would 
avoid O&M costs associated with inspecting/surveying these four mains, saving approximately $1,500 
per year. Additionally, by replacing these exposed mains, the Company could save approximately 
$150,000 every 4-5 years, which would be the cost to re-coat the main pipe and hangers for 
maintenance.  
 
The inspections performed by the Company’s Cathodic Protection group determined that long term 
solutions need to be developed and implemented in the near future to properly address the corrosion 
issues at these three locations in order to maintain system reliability and to prevent any possible future 
safety issues. In the interim, Cathodic Protection will be performing some temporary repairs by 
applying protection coating in various strategic areas until the long term, permanent solution can be 
completed. This interim work done by Cathodic Protection will not prevent this corrosion from 
recurring, it is just prolonging the service life of the pipe long enough so that a permanent solution can 
be developed and completed.   
 
This project is required in order to properly eliminate the corrosion issues at these three locations. If 
there were to be a failure in the pipe or the bridge supports, this could result in property damage, 
injuries, or fatalities to people and vehicles on the bridge as well on the thruway below the bridge. A 
Gas Distribution Event created by gas leaks caused by corrosion or outside causes is identified as a 
Corporate Risk for the Company and is a priority to be addressed.  
 
In addition, the failure of these gas mains risks the interruption of gas service in the surrounding areas, 
which could be detrimental during the cold winter months.   
 
 
 
Relationship to 5-Year and Long-Range Plans and Enterprise Risk Management Strategy 
A Gas Distribution Event created by gas leaks caused by corrosion or outside causes is identified as a 
Corporate Risk for the Company and is a priority to be addressed. If there were to be a failure in the 
pipe or the bridge supports, this could result in injuries or fatalities to people and damage to vehicles 
on the bridge as well on the thruway below the bridge. In addition, failure of these mains could also 


--- Page 88 ---
 
 
EXHIBIT____ (GIOP-1) 
 
 
PAGE 21 OF 141 
 
 
 
21 
cause the closure of the Thruway. The completion of these projects will eliminate the risk associated 
with corrosion at all three locations.  
 
 
 
2. Supplemental Information 
 
Alternatives 
 
Alternative 1 description and reason for rejection 
 
Install wax tape for the straight pipe sections except for at the supports which would need a specially 
designed coating system, such as an epoxy, to withstand the stress. This is a temporary solution. This 
will not solve the root of the problem with the corrosion and prevent further corrosion from occurring. 
This also does not address the issue with the pipe corroding at the interface of the concrete abutment 
walls. The Company would only be able to install wax tape as much as possible on the exposed and 
accessible portions of the corroded steel main and will not be able to go beyond the face of the 
abutment wall. This will not solve the problem at the interface of the abutment wall because the gas 
main pipe will continue to corrode at this point and eventually fail and shear at the interface. Based on 
this evaluation, this option was not selected.     
 
Alternative 2 description and reason for rejection 
 
Install a new Cured-In-Place liner within the existing corroded steel gas mains. This method would 
seal any existing or potential leak points within the existing gas main pipe, along with eliminating any 
internal corrosion.  Although this method would minimize excavation and restoration costs because 
the Company would only need to dig pits on either end of the gas mains to install it, and not dig up the 
entire length of pipe, it may be difficult to insert this liner through existing bends, valves, & tap holes 
within the gas pipe. More importantly, this liner relies on the existing gas main to act as a carrier pipe 
for structural support. So, although this would solve the corrosion issue with the gas main and prevent 
leaks, it does not address the issue with corroded or missing pipe hangers, and more importantly it 
does not address the issue with the pipe corrosion at the interface of the concrete abutment walls. As 
the structural integrity of the existing gas main continues to corrode, the pipe at the interface of the 
abutment wall will eventually fail and shear at the interface. Once the pipe fails at that point, there is 
nothing to structurally support the pipe at the interface of the abutment wall and thus will put stress 
on the liner and it too will eventually fail. Based on this evaluation, this option was not selected.     
 
Alternative 3 description and reason for rejection 
n/a 
 
 
Risk of No Action 
Risk 1 
A Gas Distribution Event created by gas leaks caused by corrosion or outside causes is identified as a 
Corporate Risk for the Company and is a priority to be addressed.  
 
Risk 2 
If there were to be a failure in the pipe or the bridge supports, this could result in injuries or fatalities to 
people and damage to vehicles on the bridge as well on the thruway below the bridge. In addition, 
failure of these mains could also cause the closure of the Thruway.

2. Major financial benefits

O&M costs associated with inspecting/surveying these four mains at these three locations, and it will 
save approximately $1,500 per year. By eliminating these exposed mains, the Company could save 
approximately $150,000 every 4-5 years to re-coat the main pipe and hangers for maintenance.   
 
 
3. Total cost 
$2,230,000

This project is required in order to properly eliminate the corrosion issues at these three locations. If 
there were to be a failure in the pipe or the bridge supports, this could result in injuries or fatalities to 
people and damage to vehicles on the bridge as well on the thruway below the bridge. In addition,

gas leaks caused by corrosion or outside causes is identified as a Corporate Risk for the Company and 
is a priority to be addressed. In addition, the failure of these gas mains risks the interruption of gas 
service in the surrounding areas, which could be detrimental during the cold winter months.

Gas Operations and Engineering 
 2022-2024 
 1. Project / Program Summary 
 
Type:  ☒ Project  ☐ Program  
Category:  ☒ Capital  ☐ O&M 
Work Plan Category:  ☐ Regulatory Mandated  ☒ Operationally Required  ☐ Strategic 
Project/Program Title: Elimination of Farm Taps (Extra High-Pressure Customer Service Lines)  
Project/Program Manager: David Balban 
Project/Program Number (Level 1): 
Status:  ☐ Initiation  ☐ Planning  ☐ Execution  ☒ On-going  ☐  ☐ Other: ___________ 
Estimated Start Date: 4/15/2022 
Estimated Date In Service: 12/31/2024 
I. 
Total Funding Request ($000)  
Capital: $1,200 
O&M:  
 
J. 
 
☐ 5-Year Gross Cost Savings ($000) 
☐ 5-Year Gross Cost Avoidance ($000) 
O&M: 
Capital: 
K. 5-Year Ongoing Maintenance Expense ($000) 
O&M: 
Capital: 
L. Investment Payback Period:   
(Years/months) (If applicable) 
Work Description: 
Extra high-pressure customer service lines fed from mains operating at 125 pounds per square inch 
gauge (“psig”) and above are commonly known as “farm taps.” Orange and Rockland has a 250 psig 
Maximum Allowable Operating Pressure (“MAOP”) system in Rockland and Orange Counties that 
acts like a backbone, feeding district regulator stations throughout the territory. Historically, several 
homes/services were fed directly from this system since there were no distribution mains in the 
customers’ vicinity. The objective of this work is to eliminate these extra high-pressure customer 
service lines. Farm taps represents a system risk, as each service requires two pressure reductions, with 
the first regulator either at the curb line near the main/service connection, at the building, or anywhere 
in between. If the first regulator is set at the building the customer has a 250 psig service feeding their 
building. At the end of 2021, Orange and Rockland expects that seven farm taps will remain in 
Rockland County and 22 in Orange County. The Company is targeting to remove all of the remaining 
farm taps on its system by the end of 2024.  
 
 
Justification Summary: 
Farm taps represent a safety concern and risk on the system due to extra high-pressure being fed to the 
service lines. In Orange and Rockland’s situation, the farm tap service lines are fed by a gas main with 
an MAOP of 250 psig. Pressure is reduced at the main/service connection or closer to the customer’s 
building. Since 2012 Orange and Rockland has had a concerted effort in place to eliminate farm taps. 
Most of the remaining farm taps do not have distribution main nearby and will require new mains to 
be installed to enable Orange and Rockland to transfer the service lines to the distribution system.  
 
Annually, as part of the Distribution Integrity Management Plan (“DIMP”), subject matter experts 
(“SMEs”) are required to evaluate existing and potential system threats. Farm taps have been 
determined to be an existing threat by the SMEs. Additionally, a SME Risk Ranking Analysis is 


--- Page 93 ---
 
 
EXHIBIT____ (GIOP-1) 
 
 
PAGE 26 OF 141 
 
 
 
26 
conducted as part of DIMP. Through this process the SMEs evaluate each threat that was identified, 
and rates associated frequencies of failure and consequences of failure for each threat. All results are 
risk ranked, and farm taps have rated among the top risks identified.  
 
To eliminate farm taps, installation of distribution main is required to allow the transfer of the farm tap 
service lines from extra high-pressure mains to lower pressure distribution mains. Working on extra 
high-pressure lines requires additional resources including weld inspection/x-ray, transmission grade 
materials, and specialized welders. In some cases, easements are required. Several areas have 
significant rock, and some require directional drilling.  
 
 
 
Relationship to 5-Year and Long-Range Plans and Enterprise Risk Management Strategy 
 
Elimination of farm taps is consistent with long-range plans and risk management as it increases public 
and employee safety and achieves operational excellence by improving system integrity.  
  
Elimination of farm taps is a mitigative measure identified in DIMP, and in three Enterprise Risk 
Management (ERM) programs: Gas Transmission Event, Gas Distribution Event and Gas System 
Fire/Explosion. Performance metrics for farm taps are tracked in DIMP and the ERMs and have shown 
steady progress eliminating farm taps since 2012. Performance metrics for leaks and leak rates on 
mains and services over 124 psig are tracked as well. While the number of leaks and leak rates are low 
overall, many of the leaks found by the Company on these systems are related to farm tap service lines.  
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
2. Supplemental Information 
 
Alternatives 
 
Alternative 1 description and reason for rejection 
 
Take extra mitigative actions such as increased leakage surveys, adding damage prevention signage, 
etc. These types of actions are helpful but do not solve the fundamental safety issue of having extra 
high-pressure gas in service lines next to houses or buildings. The only way to solve this is to eliminate 
the service line being fed from the 250 psig line and connect the service line to a distribution main.  
 
Alternative 2 description and reason for rejection 
n/a 
 
 
Risk of No Action 
 
Risk 1 


--- Page 94 ---
 
 
EXHIBIT____ (GIOP-1) 
 
 
PAGE 27 OF 141 
 
 
 
27 
The risk of transmission leaks rising would affect the ERM Gas Transmission Event and could have 
serious safety consequences and affect the ERM for Gas System Fire/Explosion. Additionally, not 
eliminating farm taps would be inconsistent with the Company’s DIMP which requires that mitigation 
activities are identified and employed for each existing threat. 
 
 
Risk 2 
n/a 
 
 
Risk 3 
n/a 
 
 
Non-Financial Benefits 
• 
Increased safety and reliability by reducing leaks on extra high-pressure lines  
• 
DIMP regulatory compliance is met by addressing a key system threat 
 
Summary of Financial Benefits and Costs (attach backup) 
1. Cost-benefit analysis (if required) 
n/a 
 
 
2. Major financial benefits 
n/a 
 
 
 
3. Total cost 
$1.2 million 
 
 
4. Basis for estimate 
The project estimate is based on the installation costs of past similar projects.  
 
 
5. Conclusion 
Due to the high safety risk associated with these extra high-pressure customer service lines, it is 
important the funding is provided so that this project can be completed in order to eliminate these 
services. 
 
 
Project Risks and Mitigation Plan 
 
Risk 1    
Ability to obtain easements  
Mitigation plan: starting early in the process to secure easements 
 
Risk 2   
  
 
 


--- Page 95 ---
 
 
EXHIBIT____ (GIOP-1) 
 
 
PAGE 28 OF 141

shown steady progress in eliminating farm taps since 2012. Performance metrics for leaks and leak 
rates on mains and services over 124 psig are tracked as well in DIMP and the Transmission ERM. 
While the number of leaks and leak rates are low overall, many of the leaks found by the Company on 
these systems are related to farm tap service lines.

Type:  ☒ Project  ☐ Program

Project/Program Title: Municipal Project - Congers Road, New City 
Project/Program Manager: Giovanni Coppa 
Project/Program Number (Level 1): 
Status:  ☐ Initiation  ☒ Planning  ☐ Execution  ☐ On-going  ☐  ☐ Other: ___________ 
Estimated Start Date: 1/1/2023

K. Total Funding Request ($000)  
Capital: $892.4 
O&M:  
 
L.  
☐ 5-Year Gross Cost Savings ($000) 
☐ 5-Year Gross Cost Avoidance ($000)

M. 5-Year Ongoing Maintenance Expense ($000) 
O&M: 
Capital: 
N. Investment Payback Period:   
(Years/months) (If applicable) 
Work Description: 
 
Upon notice of public improvement, city, and/or municipal construction activities, public utilities 
must relocate or protect any interfering utilities.  Whenever possible, Orange and Rockland Gas 
Engineering strategically performs advance relocation of utilities to avoid future interference with 
municipal construction projects.   
The Company has been in communication with the Rockland County Highway Department (RCHD) to 
discuss an upcoming large-scale municipal project on a one mile stretch of Congers Road between 
Strawtown Road and Brewery Road within New City in Rockland County. As part of this project, RCHD 
is planning on executing the following work:  
• 
Widening the street 
• 
Installing a new shoulder 
• 
Installing a new sidewalk 
• 
Installing new curbing 
• 
Installing new drainage structures (e.g., catch basins, manholes, and piping) 
  
RCHD has advised the Company that a large portion of its existing gas main on this roadway will 
interfere with their road work and installation of new structures. The RCHD estimates that their 
municipal project will take approximately two years to complete, with construction starting in 2022. 
 
This project will include relocating all existing gas facilities that will be interfering with RCHD’s 
municipal project. 
 
 


--- Page 98 ---
 
 
EXHIBIT____ (GIOP-1) 
 
 
PAGE 31 OF 141 
 
 
 
31 
Justification Summary: 
 
The Company is required to relocate its gas facilities wherever and whenever they are in conflict with 
municipal work being performed and when the facilities are in the municipalities’ right-of-way. This 
gas main on Congers Road is located within the Right of Way of the Municipality where their work is 
being done and therefore must be relocated. 
 
This project will provide customer satisfaction to the municipality of RCHD because they need Orange 
and Rockland to relocate any existing gas pipe that is in their way before they can install their new 
structures. Since the Company is obligated to relocate this gas pipe, the Company will also evaluate the 
gas distribution system in this area to see if this would be a good opportunity to upsize the main for 
system reinforcement. 
 
Relationship to 5-Year and Long-Range Plans and Enterprise Risk Management Strategy 
n/a 
 
 
2. Supplemental Information

activities, public utilities must relocate or protect any interfering utilities. However, Orange and 
Rockland Gas Engineering works with municipalities and offers alternatives, whenever possible, to 
avoid relocating facilities and reduce the impact of municipal work on the Company.  
 
 
Alternative 2 description and reason for rejection 
 
 
 
Alternative 3 description and reason for rejection

with municipal work being performed the Company does not have the option to take no action.

Gas Operations and Engineering 
 2022-2024 
1. Project / Program Summary 
 
Type:  ☐ Project  ☒ Program  
Category:  ☒ Capital  ☐ O&M 
Work Plan Category:  ☐ Regulatory Mandated  ☐ Operationally Required  ☒ Strategic 
Project/Program Title: AMI Natural Gas Detectors  
Project/Program Manager: Mike Turanchik 
Project/Program Number (Level 1): 
Status:  ☒ Initiation  ☐ Planning  ☐ Execution  ☐ On-going  ☐  ☐ Other: ___________ 
Estimated Start Date: 01/2022 
Estimated Date In Service: 12/2024 
M. Total Funding Request ($000)  
Capital: $630 
O&M:  
 
N.  
☐ 5-Year Gross Cost Savings ($000) 
☐ 5-Year Gross Cost Avoidance ($000) 
O&M: 
Capital: 
O. 5-Year Ongoing Maintenance Expense ($000) 
O&M: 
Capital: 
P. Investment Payback Period:   
(Years/months) (If applicable) 
Work Description: 
 
Natural Gas Detectors are safety devices installed in Customer’s indoor gas meter rooms near the head 
of service, which are intended to provide continuous monitoring of the atmosphere for a concentration 
of methane that results in an alarm. These devices are able to send alarms and events over the 
Advanced Metering Infrastructure (“AMI”) communication network. When a Natural Gas Detector 
experiences a Gas Alarm (at 10% of the Lower Explosive Limit (“LEL”)), the alarm information is 
transmitted through the AMI network to the Gas Emergency Response Center (“GERC”). GERC will 
dispatch the Gas Services Troubleshooter to respond to the potential gas leak using normal leak 
response protocols. 
 
The Company proposes to start a multi-year program to target locations within business districts to 
install these devices.  The Company estimates installing these devices in approximately 460 locations 
per year, for a total of approximately 1,400 units installed by the end of 2024 (RY3).  The estimated cost 
to purchase, install and activate the detectors is $450 per unit. 
 
Justification Summary: 
 
The Department of Public Service (DPS) has expressed interest in expanding the use of methane 
detectors. The use of methane detectors can have a positive effect on safety by providing early warning 
of methane build-up before concentrations become dangerous to life and property. The Company is 
currently involved in a pilot program installing AMI detectors and wants to expand the pilot.  Because 
these detectors alarm both locally and through the AMI system, the Company can respond to leak calls 
even if customers are not aware of the possible methane build up. 
 


--- Page 103 ---
 
 
EXHIBIT____ (GIOP-1) 
 
 
PAGE 36 OF 141 
 
 
 
36 
The installation of Natural Gas Detectors increases public and employee safety and is a mitigative 
measure in addressing Company Enterprise Risk Management (“ERMs”) such as a Gas Distribution 
Event and a Gas System Fire/Explosion.  
 
Relationship to 5-Year and Long-Range Plans and Enterprise Risk Management Strategy 
 
This program helps mitigate the danger that can result from accumulations of natural gas within 
enclosed structures, namely fires and explosions caused by natural gas. It is a mitigative measure in 
addressing Company ERMs such as a Gas Distribution Event and a Gas System Fire/Explosion.  
 
 
 
 
 
2. Supplemental Information 
 
Alternatives 
 
Alternative 1 description and reason for rejection 
 
The Company could explore other devices that could be used for early detection of indoor leaks. The 
Company is involved in several R&D initiatives which could produce promising results. These, 
however, would be long-term alternatives. The devices currently being installed are readily available, 
AMI compatible and have been tested successfully. 
 
Alternative 2 description and reason for rejection 
 
 
 
Alternative 3 description and reason for rejection 
 
 
 
Risk of No Action 
 
Risk 1 
Given that early warning and detection of leaks is important to safeguard life and property, taking no 
action with respect to this program would hinder the Company from continuing to build an indoor 
leak detection system using these devices. This program would positively impact the Company’s 
mitigation strategy for gas distribution system events, namely the risk of fire and explosion caused by 
inside leaks.

Risk 3

5. Conclusion 
The use of these devices provides the opportunity to receive leak calls sooner when the devices alarm 
alerting customers and the Company of a possible methane build up. The Orange and Rockland and 
Con Edison pilot programs have demonstrated that the AMI system is capable of generating alarms 
into emergency centers, allowing the Company to successfully use this information to respond to a 
customer location. 
 
The installation of these Natural Gas Detectors increases public and employee safety and is a 
mitigative measure in addressing Company ERMs such as a Gas Distribution Event and a Gas System 
Fire/Explosion.

Gas Operations and Engineering 
 2022-2024  
1. Project / Program Summary 
 
Type:  ☒ Project  ☐ Program  
Category:  ☒ Capital  ☐ O&M 
Work Plan Category:  ☒ Regulatory Mandated  ☒ Operationally Required  ☐ Strategic 
Project/Program Title: Monroe/KJ Reliability Project 
Project/Program Manager: David Balban 
Project/Program Number (Level 1): 
Status:  ☐ Initiation  ☒ Planning  ☐ Execution  ☐ On-going  ☐  ☐ Other: ___________ 
Estimated Start Date: 6/15/2022 
Estimated Date In Service: 12/31/2024 
O. Total Funding Request ($000)  
Capital: $2,339.6 
O&M:  
 
P.  
☐ 5-Year Gross Cost Savings ($000) 
☐ 5-Year Gross Cost Avoidance ($000) 
O&M: 
Capital: 
Q. 5-Year Ongoing Maintenance Expense ($000) 
O&M: 
Capital: 
R. Investment Payback Period:   
(Years/months) (If applicable) 
Work Description: 
 
Install approximately 6,500 feet of new 12” high density polyethylene plastic pipe on Bakertown Road 
(County Hwy 105) and Spring Street in Monroe, NY. This project will begin at the intersection of 
Spring St and Prospect St and continue to the intersection of Bakertown Road (County Hwy 105) and 
Nininger Road. Bakertown Road (County Hwy 105) is under the jurisdiction of the Orange County 
Department of Public Works and Spring Street is under the jurisdiction of the town of Monroe. It also 
crosses under Route 17 in a casing, which is under the jurisdiction of New York State Department of 
Transportation. The existing main on Bakertown Road and Spring Street is comprised of 4" steel 
(installed in 1972) and 6" steel (installed in 1994) and is currently undersized. The layout of the 
Company’s current gas infrastructure in this area cannot sufficiently provide adequate gas capacity 
from the Monroe Regulator Station to the Monroe and Kiryas Joel (KJ) gas distribution systems. 
 
 
Justification Summary: 
 
Approximately 6,500 feet of new 12” high density polyethylene plastic pipe is required on Bakertown 
Road and Spring Street in order to properly bring enough gas capacity from the Monroe Regulator 
Station to the Monroe and KJ gas distribution systems. The existing 4” and 6” pipe restricts the flow of 
gas and does not provide adequate capacity to serve these areas. This gas line on Bakertown Rd and 
Spring St is a major feed into the northern section of the Monroe gas distribution area and is one of 
only two critical feeds into the KJ gas distribution areas. Currently, there are approximately 5,750 
existing KJ customers that rely on these gas feeds for their gas distribution area, and this new 12” line 
will increase the reliability of gas service to these customers. In the event of a system interruption 
during the cold winter months, these 5,750 existing customers would experience a significant hardship. 
In order to mitigate this risk, and to provide robustness and reliability to the system, this capital project 


--- Page 108 ---
 
 
EXHIBIT____ (GIOP-1) 
 
 
PAGE 41 OF 141 
 
 
 
41 
is required. This line will also provide the capacity to serve any future load growth in the Monroe and 
KJ gas distribution areas, consistent with the Company’s statutory obligation to provide gas service. 
This project allows the Company to meet its established design criteria to provide safe and reliable gas 
service to customers within the Monroe and KJ areas. This project is also consistent with work the 
Company has performed in the past to address similarly vulnerable areas. 
 
 
 
Relationship to 5-Year and Long-Range Plans and Enterprise Risk Management Strategy 
n/a 
 
 
2. Supplemental Information 
 
Alternatives 
 
Alternative 1 description and reason for rejection 
 
An alternative to the proposed project would be to implement a non-pipes alternative project in the 
area. As documented in the Supply and Demand Analysis for Regions Vulnerable to Supply Constraints 
(filed July 17, 2020 in Case 20-G-0131), the Company explored this alternative with the largest 
developers in the area. Per the filing (page 52): “Specifically, the cross functional team – New Business, 
Distribution Planning, Customer Energy Solutions, and others – discussed the option of using heat 
pumps for both heating and cooling needs.” Ultimately, the Company was unable to secure firm 
commitments to adopt alternate heating technologies to execute a non-pipes alternative project, with 
enough lead time to meet the peak demand. Orange and Rockland will continue to leverage ongoing 
efforts such as Energy Efficiency, the Clean Heat Program, the Gas Demand Response Pilot, and the 
Heat Pump Demonstration program to explore non-pipe alternative opportunities in this area and 
throughout the territory. 
 
Alternative 2 description and reason for rejection 
 
A second alternative to the proposed project would be to tap into our existing 250# system a) to 
provide additional capacity to this area.  However, this alternative would also require the construction 
of a new district regulator station and the installation of a new line to this area. This alternative would 
be more costly and time consuming. Furthermore, this option would require added administrative 
steps, including obtaining additional permits and approvals. Therefore, it would not be cost effective 
or practical to proceed with this alternative.  
 
 
Alternative 3 description and reason for rejection 
n/a 
 
 
Risk of No Action 
 
Risk 1 
If this project is not completed, and due to the potential future growth in the area, the reliability of the 
gas distribution system in KJ would be in jeopardy, which will result in the loss of service for 
thousands of customers during the cold winter months.

is required to provide adequate gas capacity for the Monroe and KJ gas distribution areas.

Work Description: 
 
Install approximately 6,000 feet of new 12” high density polyethylene plastic pipe and 10,000 feet of

accordance with its statutory obligation to provide gas service to any customer that submits a valid 
and active application requesting such service.  
 
This project allows the Company to provide gas to this new development while meeting the 
Company’s design criteria to provide safe and reliable gas service to customers. This project is also

Gas Operations and Engineering 
 2022-2024  
1. Project / Program Summary 
 
Type:  ☒ Project  ☐ Program  
Category:  ☒ Capital  ☐ O&M 
Work Plan Category:  ☐ Regulatory Mandated  ☒ Operationally Required  ☐ Strategic 
Project/Program Title: Orangeburg Upgrade 
Project/Program Manager: David Balban 
Project/Program Number (Level 1): 
Status:  ☐ Initiation  ☐ Planning  ☐ Execution  ☒ On-going  ☐  ☐ Other: ___________ 
Estimated Start Date: 6/15/2022 
Estimated Date In Service: 12/31/2024 
S. Total Funding Request ($000)  
Capital: $650.2  
O&M:  
 
T.  
☐ 5-Year Gross Cost Savings ($000) 
☐ 5-Year Gross Cost Avoidance ($000) 
O&M: 
Capital: 
U. 5-Year Ongoing Maintenance Expense ($000) 
O&M: 
Capital: 
V. Investment Payback Period:   
(Years/months) (If applicable) 
Work Description: 
 
This project follows the Company’s strategy to standardize the Maximum Allowable Operating 
Pressure (“MAOP”) of the service territory to 80 psig to create a more integrated high-pressure system 
and improve reliability. The project consists of main replacement and pressure upgrading. 
 
Approximately 8,000 feet of 2" bare steel and 1,600 feet of 2" 1971 aldyl pipe was replaced in 
Orangeburg in the first phase. This section of Orangeburg will be upgraded to 80 pounds per square 
inch gauge (“psig”) and currently operates at medium pressure with a MAOP of 44 psig. The 
surrounding area operates at Orange and Rockland’s standard MAOP of 80 psig (except for one 
remaining adjacent section of medium pressure). In addition to replacing leak prone main and 
upgrading pressure, this project will remove 388 customers from a radial portion of the 80 psig MAOP 
system by introducing an additional feed into the area, improving system reliability for those 
customers as well as the entire area being upgraded.  
 
The project will upgrade 382 customers to 80 psig. Each customer’s regulator has been replaced with a 
bench-tested 80# regulator. Before performing the upgrade, 73 services will be replaced/re-pressure 
tested. Additionally, the Company will verify that the system to be upgraded is not connected to the 
remaining medium pressure system and will physically disconnect the two systems. Five new isolation 
valves will be installed for use in the future when the remaining 44 psig MAOP system is upgraded.   
  
Finally, this project entails performing the actual pressure upgrade. This includes raising the 44 psig 
MAOP system by four 10 psig increments and performing a leakage survey after each increase. The 
newly upgraded system will be tied in to an existing main to provide additional backfeed to this area. 
  


--- Page 118 ---
 
 
EXHIBIT____ (GIOP-1) 
 
 
PAGE 51 OF 141 
 
 
 
51 
Justification Summary: 
 
The project follows our strategy to standardize the MAOP of our service territory to 80 psig to improve 
reliability. Systematically standardizing the Orangeburg service territory will integrate the area, 
reducing pockets of single feed systems and will eventually eliminate all bare steel and aldyl in the 
area, which are classified as leak prone pipes, along with allowing the re-purposing of equipment at 
the 44 psig regulator stations. The removal of this leak prone pipe will improve pipeline integrity, 
safety, and reliability within the gas distribution system for the Company.  
 
 
Relationship to 5-Year and Long-Range Plans and Enterprise Risk Management Strategy 
The project contributes to the mitigation of Corporate Enterprise Risks such as Gas Distribution Event, 
and Large-Scale Gas Customer Outage, as well as Orange and Rockland’s Enterprise Risk of Gas 
System Fire/Explosion. 
 
 
2. Supplemental Information 
 
Alternatives 
 
Alternative 1 description and reason for rejection 
The alternative to the system upgrade is to leave the area operating at a non-standard pressure of 44 
psig MAOP. This will keep our system pressures varied, isolating small pockets of medium pressure, 
preventing integration of the 80 psig MAOP system, and leaves 388 customers on a radial feed, 
vulnerable to service interruption.  
 
Alternative 2 description and reason for rejection 
n/a 
 
Alternative 3 description and reason for rejection 
n/a 
 
 
Risk of No Action 
 
Risk 1 
If this project is not completed, it will leave 388 customers on a radial feed on the existing 80 psig
